DEPARTMENT OF WAR
OFFICE OF THE SECRETARY OF WAR/JOINT STAFF
FREEDOM OF INFORMATION
1155 DEFENSE PENTAGON
WASHINGTON, DC 20301-1155
Ref: 20-F-0163
April 22, 2026
Mr. John Greenewald
The Black Vault, Inc.
27305 W. Live Oak Road
Suite #1203
Castaic, CA 91384
Dear Mr. Greenewald:
This is a final response to your October 29, 2019 Freedom of Information Act (FOIA)
request, a copy of which is enclosed for your convenience. The Office of the Secretary of
War/Joint Staff (OSW/JS) FOIA Requester Service Center received your request on
October 30, 2019, and assigned it FOIA case number 20-F-0163. We ask that you use this
number when referring to your request.
The Office of the Assistant to the Secretary of War for Public Affairs (OATSW(PA)), а
component of OSW, conducted a search of their records systems and located 416 pages
determined to be responsive to your request. Ms. Tanya Rose, Information Management
Director, OATSW(PA); Mr. David Bennett, Director, DoD Office of Information Counsel Office
of General Counsel; C.C. Davison, Chief, Records and Open Government, Defense Intelligence
Agency; Mr. Mark Dorgan, Director, FOIA, Privacy and Civil Liberties Office, Department of
Defense Office of Inspector General; and Mr. Richard Strong, Director Navy FOIA/PA Program
Office, in their capacities as Initial Denial Authorities, have determined that portions of the 416
responsive pages are exempt from release pursuant to 5 U.S.C. § 552 (b)(3), information
specifically exempted by a statute establishing particular criteria for withholding, applying
10 U.S.C. § 424 which protects an organization or any function of an organization, and certain
information pertaining to, employees of the Defense Intelligence Agency, the National
Reconnaissance Office, and the National Geospatial-Intelligence Agency. Information was also
withheld pursuant to 5 U.S.C. § 552 (b)(5), inter- and intra- agency memoranda which are
deliberative in nature; this exemption is appropriate for internal documents which are part of the
decision-making process, and contain subjective evaluations, opinions and recommendations;
(b)(6), information which, if disclosed, would constitute a clearly unwarranted invasion of the
personal privacy of individuals. Please note that we have considered the foreseeable harm
standard when reviewing records and applying exemptions under the FOIA in the processing of
this request.
In this instance, fees for processing your request were below the threshold for requiring
payment. Please note that fees may be assessed on future requests.

If you have any questions or concerns about the foregoing or about the processing of your
request, please do not hesitate to contact the Action Officer assigned to your request, Brianna
Johnson, at brianna.n.johnson2.civ@mail.mil or 571-372-0409. Additionally, if you have
concerns about service received by our office, please contact a member of our Leadership Team
at 571-372-0498 or Toll Free at 866-574-4970.
Should you wish to inquire about mediation services, you may contact the OSW/JS FOIA
Public Liaison, Virginia Burke, by email at osd.mc-alex.oatsd-pclt.mbx.foia-liaison@mail.mil or
by phone at 571-372-0462. You may also contact the Office of Government Information
Services (OGIS) at the National Archives and Records Administration to inquire about the FOIA
mediation services they offer. The contact information for OGIS is as follows:
Office of Government Information Services
National Archives and Records Administration
8601 Adelphi Road-OGIS
College Park, MD 20740
E-mail: ogis@nara.gov
Telephone: 202-741-5770
Fax: 202-741-5769
Toll-free: 1-877-684-6448
You have the right to appeal to the appellate authority, Mr. Michael Kremlacek, Acting
Principal Deputy to the Secretary of War for Privacy, Civil Liberties, and Transparency (PCLT),
Office of the Secretary of War, at: 4800 Mark Center Drive, ATTN: PCLFD, FOIA Appeals,
Mailbox #24, Alexandria, VA 22350-1700. Your appeal must be postmarked within 90 calendar
days of the date of this response. Alternatively, you may email your appeal to osd.foia-
appeal@mail.mil. If you use email, please include the words "FOIA Appeal" in the subject of the
email. Please also reference FOIA case number 20-F-0163 in any appeal correspondence.
We appreciate your patience in the processing of your request. As stated previously,
please contact the Action Officer assigned to your request, Brianna Johnson, and reference FOIA
case number 20-F-0163, if you have any questions or concerns.

{"alpha_words": 345, "chars": 2566, "method": "ocr", "page": 12, "variant": "plain-vision"}
HARRY REID
NEVADA
MAJORITY LEADER
Lanited States Senate
WASHINGTON, DC 20510-7012
June 24, 2009
Honorable William Lynn III
Deputy Secretary of Defense
1010 Defense Pentagon
Washington, DC 20301-1010
Dear Secretary Lynn:
Beginning this past September, the U.S. Senate has mandated that the Defense Intelligence
Agency assess far-term foreign advanced aerospace threats to the United States. The scope of
program interest covers from the present out to forty years and beyond. In order to further our
effort in recognizing emerging disruptive aerospace technologies, technical studies are being
conducted in regard to advanced lift, propulsion, the use of unconventional materials and
controls, signature reduction, weaponry, human interface and human effects.
Since the Advanced Aerospace Threat and Identification Program (AAITP) and study were
first commissioned, much progress has been made with the identification of several highly
sensitive, unconventional aerospace-related findings. Given the current rate of success, the
continued study of these subjects will likely lead to technology advancements that in the
immediate near-torm will require extraordinary protection. Due to the sensitivities of the
information surrounding aspects of this program, I require your assistance in establishing a
Restricted Special-Access-Program (SAP) with a Bigoted Access List for specific portions of the
AATIP.
In order to support this national effort, a small but highly specialized cadre of Department of
Defense (DoD) and private sector individuals are necessary. These individuals must be
specialized in the areas of advanced sciences, sensors, intelligence/counterintelligence, and
advanced aerospace engineering. Given the likelihood that these technologies will be applied to
future systems involving space flight, weapons, communications, and propulsion, the standard
management and safeguarding procedures for classified information are not sufficient. Even the
use of conventional SAP protocols will not adequately ensure that all aspects of the project are
properly secured. Although not every aspect of AATIP requires Restricted SAP read-on, the
following portions should be maintained at the Restricted SAP level:
• The methodology used to identify, acquire, study, and engineer the advanced
technologies associated with AATIP.
• Specific methodologies used to study unconventional technology inay require
nuanced approaches that will undoubtedly be of significant interest if not a top
priority for adversarial Foreign Intelligence Security Services (FISS).

===== PAGE 13 =====
{"alpha_words": 349, "chars": 2516, "method": "ocr", "page": 13, "variant": "plain-vision"}
Undue attention by government, or private sector entities, not involved in AATIP
or any international interest will directly or indirectly interfere with the daily
AATIP mission and perhaps threaten the overall success of the program.
• Allocation of personnel, support, and oversight.
• Due to the highly specialized nature of the personnel involved with AATIP, the
overt acknowledgement of their participation in the program will lead to an
unnecessary security and counterintelligence risk.
Occasional assistance from specialized individuals within DoD, the scientific
community, or academia may be necessary from time to time based on
demonstrated subject matter expertise. Adequate protection of their identities or
affiliation is critical to avoid unnecessary scrutiny.
0
Without the appropriate Restricted SAP protection, the cost associated with a
compromise would be significantly higher than the cost associated with a
properly administered Restricted SAP.
• Protection of industry partnerships and participation is critical. Public
awareness of an industry's AATIP affiliation may discourage that industry's
further participation with the U.S. Government in this program.
• Application and engineering.
o The nuanced manner in which some of these technologies will be collected,
engineered and applied by the U.S. may require senior level government approval.
These decision makers must be afforded the necessary time to make strategic
decisions by restricting aceess to the "big picture" or overall intent of the program
to those on a strict Bigoted List.
• Associated exotic technologies likely involve extremely sophisticated concepts
within the world of quantum mechanics, nuclear science, electromagnetic theory,
gravitics, and thermodynamics. Given that all of these have the potential to be
used with catastrophic effects by adversaries, an unusually high degree of
operational security and read-on discretion is required.
Due to the expertise required to carry out the objectives of this program, we will require a
small, specialized group of DoD personnel, who are dedicated to performing the SAP-related
functions and executing programmatic requirements within the program. It is essential that the
Government & military personnel who are already involved with this program are assigned to
further support this program in a Restricted SAP capacity (see Attachment 1). These individuals
all currently possess the appropriate security clearances and are already providing unique support
to AATIP.

{"alpha_words": 345, "chars": 2566, "method": "ocr", "page": 26, "variant": "plain-vision"}
HARRY REID
NEVADA
MAJORITY LEADER
Lanited States Senate
WASHINGTON, DC 20510-7012
June 24, 2009
Honorable William Lynn III
Deputy Secretary of Defense
1010 Defense Pentagon
Washington, DC 20301-1010
Dear Secretary Lynn:
Beginning this past September, the U.S. Senate has mandated that the Defense Intelligence
Agency assess far-term foreign advanced aerospace threats to the United States. The scope of
program interest covers from the present out to forty years and beyond, In order to further our
effort in recognizing emerging disruptive aerospace technologies, technical studies are being
conducted in regard to advanced lift, propulsion, the use of unconventional materials and
controls, signature reduction, weaponry, human interface and human effects.
Since the Advanced Aerospace Threat and Identification Program (AAITP) and study were
first commissioned, much progress has been made with the identification of several highly
sensitive, unconventional aerospace-related findings. Given the current rate of success, the
continued study of these subjects will likely lead to technology advancements that in the
immediate near-torm will require extraordinary protection. Due to the sensitivities of the
information surrounding aspects of this program, I require your assistance in establishing a
Restricted Special-Access-Program (SAP) with a Bigoted Access List for specific portions of the
AATIP.
In order to support this national effort, a small but highly specialized cadre of Department of
Defense (DoD) and private sector individuals are necessary, These individuals must be
specialized in the areas of advanced sciences, sensors, intelligence/counterintelligence, and
advanced aerospace engineering. Given the likelihood that these technologies will be applied to
future systems involving space flight, weapons, communications, and propulsion, the standard
management and safeguarding procedures for classified information are not sufficient. Even the
use of conventional SAP protocols will not adequately ensure that all aspects of the project are
properly secured. Although not every aspect of AATIP requires Restricted SAP read-on, the
following portions should be maintained at the Restricted SAP level:
• The methodology used to identify, acquire, study, and engineer the advanced
technologies associated with AATIP.
• Specific methodologies used to study unconventional technology inay require
nuanced approaches that will undoubtedly be of significant interest if not a top
priority for adversarial Foreign Intelligence Security Services (FISS).

===== PAGE 27 =====
{"alpha_words": 349, "chars": 2516, "method": "ocr", "page": 27, "variant": "plain-vision"}
Undue attention by government, or private sector entities, not involved in AATIP
or any international interest will directly or indirectly interfere with the daily
AATIP mission and perhaps threaten the overall success of the program.
• Allocation of personnel, support, and oversight.
• Due to the highly specialized nature of the personnel involved with AATIP, the
overt acknowledgement of their participation in the program will lead to an
unnecessary security and counterintelligence risk.
Occasional assistance from specialized individuals within DoD, the scientific
community, or academia may be necessary from time to time based on
demonstrated subject matter expertise. Adequate protection of their identities or
affiliation is critical to avoid unnecessary scrutiny.
0
Without the appropriate Restricted SAP protection, the cost associated with a
compromise would be significantly higher than the cost associated with a
properly administered Restricted SAP.
• Protection of industry partnerships and participation is critical. Public
awareness of an industry's AATIP affiliation may discourage that industry's
further participation with the U.S. Government in this program.
• Application and engineering.
o The nuanced manner in which some of these technologies will be collected,
engineered and applied by the U.S. may require senior level government approval.
These decision makers must be afforded the necessary time to make strategic
decisions by restricting aceess to the "big picture" or overall intent of the program
to those on a strict Bigoted List.
• Associated exotic technologies likely involve extremely sophisticated concepts
within the world of quantum mechanics, nuclear science, electromagnetic theory,
gravitics, and thermodynamics. Given that all of these have the potential to be
used with catastrophic effects by adversaries, an unusually high degree of
operational security and read-on discretion is required.
Due to the expertise required to carry out the objectives of this program, we will require a
small, specialized group of DoD personnel, who are dedicated to performing the SAP-related
functions and executing programmatic requirements within the program. It is essential that the
Government & military personnel who are already involved with this program are assigned to
further support this program in a Restricted SAP capacity (see Attachment 1). These individuals
all currently possess the appropriate security clearances and are already providing unique support
to AATIP.

public seal in inades or use his ed seria treate false contin are personal all
the potential to profoundly damage my career and with it my ability to provide for my wife and

In that regard, attached you will find a sample of unclassified correspondence that clearly
demonstrates how active 1 remained on the UAP issue right up until my departure, to include
interagency coordination with the IC on this important issue and operational collaboration with the
US Navy. The transition plans referenced herein were briefed to and approved by a senior political
appointee who reported directly to General Mattis. Some of the F-18 pilots and Navy officials can
confirm multiple briefings and discussions with this individual and others, to include
Congress. Some of these discussions and briefings actually occurred inside the SecDef's personal
office suite. That is why my emails reference "the front office."
Officials from other agencies and departments (ie DIA and CIA) attended meetings I held to
coordinate activities on this topic in support of the men and women in the US Navy, who as you
know were and still are deeply perplexed and concerned by this phenomenon. AATIP was an official
program that was never shut down, when the funding dried-up I continued to lead and interact with

===== PAGE 33 =====
{"alpha_words": 668, "chars": 3939, "method": "ocr", "page": 33, "variant": "plain-vision"}
officials in DoD and other agencies to the best of my ability in the interest of national security. When
1 departed, as is clear from my resignation letter, it was purely out of frustration with my ability to gain
additional traction and support. But 1 put my shoulder to the wheel all those years and worked
diligently to maintain a focus on the AATIP issue and continued to coordinate and promote collection
and analysis. Call it an "activity" vs a "program" if you will, but if this escalates it will be easy to
prove how active I remained in support of our men and women in uniform, especially in the US
Navy. If some who were not privy to my actions want to portray me as a rogue, well bad on them for
sitting on their hands and doing nothing about recurring intrusions into US airspace and military
operating areas and good on me for continuing to risk my reputation and career in support of the US
Navy and the nation. Personally, it is outrageous so many others did so little in the face of what the
Navy now publicly acknowiedges is serious national security issue. The plain truth is that I did
everything I could to maintain focus and attention on this vital issue when so many others continued
to sweep it under the rug purely for fear of the stigma and possible impact on their reputations. And
for that the department wants to make me out to be the bad guy? An imposter? For showing
initiative and support to the Navy and others? For keeping this effort going as best I could -- an
effort now fully vindicated by the Navy itself?
As you can well imagine, 1am not only hurt and disappointed but angry. I should if anything be
getting accolades for persistence and initiative, especially since the Navy has now publicly
acknowledged the seriousness of the issue, something that only occurred because of my efforts.
Instead, a knife in the back? Wow, thanks guys! If the department wants to have this out in public
we can certainly do so. I can easily establish that Neil took the meetings referenced in these emails
and that this plan was blessed by an individual who has departed but was at the time a senior
Administration appointee and directly reported to General Mattis. Do you honestly think I made up
the stuff in these emails? Really? Check Neil's calendars and you will see these meetings occurred
(as well as innumerable other meetings on my calendars)-
1am providing these documents to you to afford you and the Department a final opportunity to
correct the record. Meanwhile, I am being pushed into a corner as you can well imagine and will
soon be forced to engage an attorney and respond. The oversight committees are also well aware
of my role and 1 believe appreciative of my efforts.
In conclusion, it seems clear to me and others that there exists an extreme miscommunication
among OUSDI staff. I truly hope we can resolve this amicably.
What a sad statement it is that instead of being rewarded for my initiative and persistence, for
diligently pursuing an issue so many others were willing to duck, some in the department are instead
trying to destroy my career and reputation by sowing disinformation. To reiterate, I was an OUSD!
staff member while working with the AATIP portfolio up until the point of my departure. This was
previously verified by the DoD Press Secretary in 2017. Check Neil's calendar and you will be able
to confirm these meetings occurred - a small sample of the total number of meetings I was having
on a weekly and near daily basis at the time.
I know that you are trying to be a fair and honest broker. I hope you will therefore take this letter in
the spirit of a former colleague merely seeking to set the record straight.

is growing.

Lue Elizondo

Hi Chris:
I am reaching out in a final effort to avoid seeing this unfortunate situation escalate. As a soldier and
public servant with decades of unblemished service,
as yic ear well imagine. oreover, the clearly a tree nor one reyeror ea
the potential to profoundly damage my career and with it my ability to provide for my wife and family.
In that regard, attached you will find a sample of unclassified correspondence that clearly
demonstrates how active I remained on the UAP issue right up until my departure, to include
interagency coordination with the IC on this important issue and operational collaboration with the
US Navy. The transition plans referenced herein were briefed to and approved by a senior political
appointee who reported directly to General Mattis. Some of the F-18 pilots and Navy officials can
confirm multiple briefings and discussions with this individual and others, to include
Congress.
Some of these discussions and briefings actually occurred inside the SecDef's personal
office suite. That is why my emails reference "the front office."
Officials from other agencies and departments (ie DIA and CIA) attended meetings I held to
coordinate activities on this topic in support of the men and women in the US Navy, who as you
know were and still are deeply perplexed and concerned by this phenomenon. AATIP was an official
program that was never shut down, when the funding dried-up continued to lead and interact with
officials in DoD and other agencies to the best of my ability in the interest of national security. When
I departed, as is clear from my resignation letter, it was purely out of frustration with my ability to gain
additional traction and support. But I put my shoulder to the wheel all those years and worked
diligently to maintain a focus on the AATIP issue and continued to coordinate and promote collection
and analysis. Call it an "activity" vs a "program" if you will, but if this escalates it will be easy to
prove how active I remained in support of our men and women in uniform, especially in the US
Navy. If some who were not privy to my actions want to portray me as a rogue, well bad on them for
sitting on their hands and doing nothing about recurring intrusions into US airspace and military
operating areas and good on me for continuing to risk my reputation and career in support of the US
Navy and the nation. Personally, it is outrageous so many others did so little in the face of what the
Navy now publicly acknowledges is serious national security issue. The plain truth is that I did
everything I could to maintain focus and attention on this vital issue when so many others continued
to sweep it under the rug purely for fear of the stigma and possible impact on their reputations. And
for that the department wants to make me out to be the bad guy? An imposter? For showing
initiative and support to the Navy and others? For keeping this effort going as best I could - an
effort now fully vindicated by the Navy itself?
As you can well imagine, I am not only hurt and disappointed but angry. I should if anything be
getting accolades for persistence and initiative, especially since the Navy has now publicly
acknowledged the seriousness of the issue, something that only occurred because of my efforts.
Instead, a knife in the back? Wow, thanks guys! If the department wants to have this out in public
we can certainly do so. I can easily establish that Neil took the meetings referenced in these emails
and that this plan was blessed by an individual who has departed but was at the time a senior
Administration appointee and directly reported to General Mattis. Do you honestly think I made up

(b)(6)
Establishment of Unidentified Aerial Phenomena Task Force
Friday, August 14, 2020 6:13:41 PM
Media colleagues,
On Aug. 4, 2020, Deputy Secretary of Defense David L. Norquist approved the establishment of an
Unidentified Aerial Phenomena (UAP) Task Force (UAPTF). The Department of the Navy, under the
cognizance of the Office of the Under Secretary of Defense for Intelligence and Security, will lead the
UAPTF.
The Department of Defense established the UAPTF to improve its understanding of, and gain insight
into, the nature and origins of UAPs. The mission of the task force is to detect, analyze and catalog
UAPs that could potentially pose a threat to U.S. national security.
As DOD has stated previously, the safety of our personnel and the security of our operations are of
paramount concern. The Department of Defense and the military departments take any incursions
by unauthorized aircraft into our training ranges or designated airspace very seriously and examine
each report. This includes examinations of incursions that are initially reported as UAP when the
observer cannot immediately identify what he or she is observing.
Regards,
(b)(6)
(b)(6)

{"alpha_words": 345, "chars": 2566, "method": "ocr", "page": 78, "variant": "plain-vision"}
HARRY REID
NEVADA
MAJORITY LEADER
Lanited States Senate
WASHINGTON, DC 20510-7012
June 24, 2009
Honorable William Lynn III
Deputy Secretary of Defense
1010 Defense Pentagon
Washington, DC 20301-1010
Dear Secretary Lynn:
Beginning this past September, the U.S. Senate has mandated that the Defense Intelligence
Agency assess far-term foreign advanced aerospace threats to the United States. The scope of
program interest covers from the present out to forty years and beyond, In order to further our
effort in recognizing emerging disruptive aerospace technologies, technical studies are being
conducted in regard to advanced lift, propulsion, the use of unconventional materials and
controls, signature reduction, weaponry, human interface and human effects.
Since the Advanced Aerospace Threat and Identification Program (AAITP) and study were
first commissioned, much progress has been made with the identification of several highly
sensitive, unconventional aerospace-related findings. Given the current rate of success, the
continued study of these subjects will likely lead to technology advancements that in the
immediate near-torm will require extraordinary protection. Due to the sensitivities of the
information surrounding aspects of this program, I require your assistance in establishing a
Restricted Special-Access-Program (SAP) with a Bigoted Access List for specific portions of the
AATIP.
In order to support this national effort, a small but highly specialized cadre of Department of
Defense (DoD) and private sector individuals are necessary, These individuals must be
specialized in the areas of advanced sciences, sensors, intelligence/counterintelligence, and
advanced aerospace engineering. Given the likelihood that these technologies will be applied to
future systems involving space flight, weapons, communications, and propulsion, the standard
management and safeguarding procedures for classified information are not sufficient. Even the
use of conventional SAP protocols will not adequately ensure that all aspects of the project are
properly secured. Although not every aspect of AATIP requires Restricted SAP read-on, the
following portions should be maintained at the Restricted SAP level:
• The methodology used to identify, acquire, study, and engineer the advanced
technologies associated with AATIP.
• Specific methodologies used to study unconventional technology inay require
nuanced approaches that will undoubtedly be of significant interest if not a top
priority for adversarial Foreign Intelligence Security Services (FISS).

===== PAGE 79 =====
{"alpha_words": 349, "chars": 2516, "method": "ocr", "page": 79, "variant": "plain-vision"}
Undue attention by government, or private sector entities, not involved in AATIP
or any international interest will directly or indirectly interfere with the daily
AATIP mission and perhaps threaten the overall success of the program.
• Allocation of personnel, support, and oversight.
• Due to the highly specialized nature of the personnel involved with AATIP, the
overt acknowledgement of their participation in the program will lead to an
unnecessary security and counterintelligence risk.
Occasional assistance from specialized individuals within DoD, the scientific
community, or academia may be necessary from time to time based on
demonstrated subject matter expertise. Adequate protection of their identities or
affiliation is critical to avoid unnecessary scrutiny.
0
Without the appropriate Restricted SAP protection, the cost associated with a
compromise would be significantly higher than the cost associated with a
properly administered Restricted SAP.
• Protection of industry partnerships and participation is critical. Public
awareness of an industry's AATIP affiliation may discourage that industry's
further participation with the U.S. Government in this program.
• Application and engineering.
o The nuanced manner in which some of these technologies will be collected,
engineered and applied by the U.S. may require senior level government approval.
These decision makers must be afforded the necessary time to make strategic
decisions by restricting aceess to the "big picture" or overall intent of the program
to those on a strict Bigoted List.
• Associated exotic technologies likely involve extremely sophisticated concepts
within the world of quantum mechanics, nuclear science, electromagnetic theory,
gravitics, and thermodynamics. Given that all of these have the potential to be
used with catastrophic effects by adversaries, an unusually high degree of
operational security and read-on discretion is required.
Due to the expertise required to carry out the objectives of this program, we will require a
small, specialized group of DoD personnel, who are dedicated to performing the SAP-related
functions and executing programmatic requirements within the program. It is essential that the
Government & military personnel who are already involved with this program are assigned to
further support this program in a Restricted SAP capacity (see Attachment 1). These individuals
all currently possess the appropriate security clearances and are already providing unique support
to AATIP.

{"alpha_words": 251, "chars": 1763, "method": "ocr", "page": 84, "variant": "plain-vision"}
The Advanced Aviation Threat Identification Program:
The Advanced Aviation Threat Identification Program ended in 2012. It was determined that
there were other, higher priority issues that merited funding and it was in the best interest of the DoD
to make a change.
•
The DoD takes seriously all threats and potential threats to our people, our assets, and our
mission and takes action whenever credible information is developed.
•
The Department of Defense is always concerned about maintaining positive identification of all
aircraft in our operating environment as well as identifying any foreign capability that may be a threat to
the homeland.
The department will continue to investigate, through normal procedures, reports of
unidentified aircraft encountered by US military aviators in order to ensure defense of the homeland
and protection against strategic surprise by our nation's adversaries.
New Navy reporting procedures:
The Navy has updated and formalized the process by which reports of any such suspected
incursions of unauthorized and/or unidentified aircraft entering various military-controlled ranges and
designated air space can be made to the cognizant authorities.
For safety and security concerns, the Navy takes these reports very seriously and investigates
each and every report.

of the word and spirit of the arms embargo. The United States prioritizes finding an inclusive,
negotiated, political solution to the Libyan conflict."

(b)(5)
QUERIES OF NOTE
* 2020 Recruitment Figures. Andrew Jeong, WSJ, asked a series of questions on whether the
2020 pandemic and its adverse effects on the job market has led to more applicants to the
Armed Forces, compared to 2019, and previous recent years: was there an increase; how
much did application numbers jump during the 2007-09 financial crisis; how many applications
did the four branches and the Coast Guard receive in 2005, 2006, 2007, 2008, and 2009; and
would there be a rough percentage based statistic available. Coordinating response with
Manpower and Reserve Affairs Office.
* UAPTF Budget. Duncan Phenix, Mystery Wire, 8 News Now (Las Vegas), asked whether a
statement on Twitter attributed to a DoD spokesperson was accurate, and asked about the
budget for the UAPTF. Confirmed the accuracy of the statement. Coordinating with Navy and
OUSD(I&S) on the budget questions.
* UAP Report to Congress. Douglas Johnson, Scientific Coalition for UAP Studies/TheDebrief,
asked for information regarding the role of the Department of Defense in responding to the
congressional directive, with regard to process, timing, or any other aspect. Responded that
we had nothing further beyond what ODNI had provided him (which was simply that ODNI is
aware of the requirement and will respond accordingly).
* Tenant Bill of Rights Status. Haley Britzky, Task and Purpose, asked about the status of the
privatized housing Tenant Bill of Rights, where four - maintenance history, dispute resolution,
rent withholding, and the right to common documents- had not been implemented yet and
were still being worked on. Coordinated with DASD(Housing) that the Department has been
working with the MHPI companies to gain their voluntary agreement to retroactively apply the
remaining requirements to existing MHPI projects. Based on those ongoing discussions, we
expect the remaining four tenant rights to be available at the majority of vast installations with
MHPI housing by June 2021.
* Service Member Financial Benefits. Brenden Middleton, The Elks Magazine, requested
information on financial benefits for service members. He wanted to know the number of
service members who take advantage of financial benefits and what kind of benefits are
available to spouses. Coordinating with MPP (M&RA): There are several dedicated sites that
list military pay and benefits such as Military OneSource, the Department of Defense Office of

===== PAGE 98 =====
{"alpha_words": 462, "chars": 3105, "method": "ocr", "page": 98, "variant": "plain-vision"}
Financial Readiness (FINRED), the Department of Defense Military Spouse Employment
Partnership (MSEP), etc. It is important to the DoD to sustain the All-Volunteer Force and
support Service members and their families. DoD utilizes its full array of pay and benefits in
order to recruit, retain, develop, motivate and assign Service members to locations around the
world and to support them and their families. The military has a pay and benefits package
that compares very favorably to the private sector in recognition of the dedication and
sacrifices our military members make in service to the nation. Amid the pandemic, the Office
of the Under Secretary of Defense for Personnel and Readiness issued military personnel
guidance addressing special policies and procedures implemented to ensure continuing
financial security for our members and their families in response to COVID-19.
* GAO Report on BAH. Rose Thayer, Stars and Stripes, requested a comment on the recent
report on BAH from the GAO. Response coordinated with M&RA and provided to the
reporter: "We appreciate GAO's thorough review of the BAH program and are gratified they
found the program to be sound and well run. We welcome their recommendations for
improvements to ensure this program better and more accurately reflects the housing costs
faced by our Service members and their families. The Military Compensation Policy staff
continuously reviews the BAH program and identifies areas for potential process
improvement. The GAO report's final recommendations are largely consistent with
improvements Military Compensation also identified for implementation in coming years. We
look forward to finding the best implementation solutions for these recommendations."
* EO on Transgender Service Members. Chris Johnson, Washington Blade, asked if discharges
under DTM-19-004 now prohibited and will be prohibited as long as the EO is in place;
whether the Department think it must implement a policy allowing individuals who obtained
treatment for gender dysphoria to accede or enlist; and whether government funds will be
used to pay for transition-related care, as well as the number of discharges and blocked
accessions under DTM-19-004. Response coordinated with M&RA and provided to the
reporter: "While the Department appreciates your interest and questions, it would be
premature to comment on prospective policy at this time. We refer you to the Executive
Order, the White House Fact Sheet, and Secretary Austin's statement for now and commit to
providing updated information as it becomes available. As you will note, the Secretary's
statement calls for 60 days to "develop the appropriate policies and procedures to implement
these changes.
* Letter to Evolution Metals Corporation. Jacob Holman, S&P Global Market Intelligence, and
James Munson, Bloomberg Environment, asked whether a letter allegedly signed by
representatives from DOE, DOC, and DOD and sent to Evolution Metals Corporation is
authentic, as well as whether the letter received an ethics review by the Department before
the DoD representative signed.

conditions are fully met. A conditions-based wartime OPCON transition is not only what the

peoples, and the region requires is more complicated than merely swapping the leadership of

CORRECTION: Regarding the 5G network at Nellis AFB, it's OUSD(R&E) teaming with USAFWC, not

ON TOP
* SECDEF Virtual Town Hall. Secretary Esper, CJCS Gen. Milley, and SEAC Colón-López hosted a global
virtual town hall to answer questions from service members, DoD civilians, and family members
about COVID-19. Topics discussed included vaccine development, cleaning of workspaces,
discussions between supervisors and employees on returning to offices, coronavirus antibody
testing, donating plasma and blood (antibodies), potential reduction of quarantine timelines,
hazardous duty pay and awards, military allowances, stop-move, DoD budget, training guidelines,
Reserve and National Guard deployments, retrieving/shipping personal vehicles, stress on military
families, and appeals to denials of hardship exceptions.
* B1 Presence in South China Sea. Received multiple queries overnight from Korean and Japanese
outlets on the B1 presence in the SCS. Responded with the following on-record: "The U.S. military
conducts training all over the world, all the time. Training is scheduled months in advance, often
done with our allies and partners in the region, and it's essential to ensure we remain the greatest,
most powerful, and brilliant fighting force the world has ever known. Additionally, the U.5. military
will continue to fly, sail, and operate anywhere international law allows."
DOD RELEASES
* 5G Network at Nellis. Announced that OUSD(R&E) and USAW at Nellis AFB have teamed to build
a 5G cellular network on Nellis as part of DOD's development of 5G for both defense and civilian
uses. Only users taking part in the testing will have access to the private network. The network will
feature relocatable cell towers that can be set up and taken down in less than an hour. Testing will
involve mobile operations centers where team members will use the network while on the move.
Construction on the network at Nellis starts in July; testing at Nellis will start in January 2021 and
continue in three 12-month phases. This test builds upon DoD's previously announced 5G
communications technology prototyping and experimentation at Hill AFB, Utah; Joint Base Lewis-
McChord, Washington; Marine Corps Logistics Base Albany, Georgia; and Naval Base San Diego,
California.
* DPA Title 3 COVID-19 PPE Project. Announced that DoD, in support of HHS, has signed a $2.2
million contract with Hollingsworth & Vose to increase U.S. domestic production of 27.5 million N95
ventilator filters, and 3.1 million N95 respirators per month, starting in August. This investment will
increase U.S. domestic production of N95 mask respirator and mask ventilator filter production by
over 30 million combined over the next 120 days.

* Air Strikes in Africa, Mike Stone, Reuters, queried for a list of African countries in which the U.S.

* Russian Aircraft in Libya. Ashley Roque, Janes, queried for additional information regarding the
U.S. Africa Command press release that Russia is supplying aircraft in Libya. Coordinated with
AFRICOM to respond: Over multiple days in May, Russian MiG 29s and SU-24 fighters departed
Russia. At that time, all the Russian aircraft have Russian Federation Air Force markings. The newly
unmarked Russian MiG 29s then depart Syria. They are flown by Russian military members and
escorted by Russian fighters based in Syria, head to Libya, landing in Eastern Libya near Tobruk for
fuel. The MiG-29s are then delivered to Al Jufra Air Base in Libya, where they will likely provide close
air support and offensive fires for the Russian state-sponsored Wagner Group. There were at least
14 aircraft delivered.
* Ukraine Security Assistance. Luis Martinez, ABC, asked about Ukraine security assistance.
Responded that we do not speculate on security assistance packages that are under review with
Congress.

the Indo-Pacific's role as the priority theater." PDI and other decisions have demonstrated that
we're putting more and more resources toward the Indo-Pacific. "Trend lines are positive, but it will
be a slug, particularly in the COVID environment."

* Navy UAP Video Release. Released a statement (below) on the official release of three Navy
UAP videos. The statement and the posting of the videos on the NAVAIR FOIA Reading Room
generated queries about the videos and the old AATIP program throughout the day from
multiple outlets. CNN, ABC, CBS, Task and Purpose, Washington Examiner, Military.com and
other outlets ran stories. Provided clarification to Washington Examiner on a common
misconception in their article, and the reporter updated it immediately. Statement: "The
Department of Defense has authorized the release of three unclassified Navy videos, one
taken in November 2004 and the other two in January 2015, which have been circulating in
the public domain after unauthorized releases in 2007 and 2017. The U.S. Navy previously
acknowledged that these videos circulating in the public domain were indeed Navy videos.
After a thorough review, the department has determined that the authorized release of these
unclassified videos does not reveal any sensitive capabilities or systems, and does not impinge
on any subsequent investigations of military air space incursions by unidentified aerial
phenomena. DOD is releasing the videos in order to clear up any misconceptions by the public
on whether or not the footage that has been circulating was real, or whether or not there is
more to the videos. The aerial phenomena observed in the videos remain characterized as
"unidentified." The released videos can be found at the Naval Air Systems Command FOIA
Reading Room: https://www.navair.navy.mil/foia/documents."
* DoD Small Business Webinar Series. Released an announcement on the third Defense Small

Investment: Tools for Small Businesses and How DoD Can Help." The release noted that the

* Open Skies Aircraft. Joseph Trevithick, The War Zone, asked, among other things, whether or not
any OC-135 aircraft with the DVIS upgrade had conducted any missions under the Open Skies Treaty.
Responded: "The Digital Visual Imaging System (DVIS) is designed to replace the current wet film
camera system on board the U.S. Open Skies Aircraft. The 55th Wing maintains one Treaty certified
wet-film aircraft to conduct Open Skies missions and one aircraft with the DVIS system installed. The
aircraft with DVIS installed is currently in the test phase, and has not been certified for use on Open
Skies missions."
* Ligado. Dee Ann Divis, Inside GNSS/Inside Unmanned Systems, queried about whether the U.S.

network in spectrum adjacent to GPS. She also asked about the impact to the U.S. military should
Ligado be allowed to implement its proposal globally. Provided that DOD is concerned about the
Ligado request, remains opposed to it and that the impact on legacy military receivers used by DOD
wodl require modification or replacement of this equipment across the department, something that
would cost billions of dollars and take many years to complete. Any global terrestrial mobile
broadband deployment in the frequency band under consideration would adversely impact military
use of GPS.

Times,
asked for a statement on the presentation of the first Purple Hearts from the Jan. 8, 2020,
theater ballistic missile attack at Al Asad. CENTCOM provided the following statement: "The first six
Purple Hearts approved for injuries sustained during a Jan. 8, 2020 Iranian ballistic missile attack on
Al Assad Air Base in Iraq were awarded to U.S. Army soldiers in Iraq and Kuwait on May 3 and 4,
respectively. The awards were the first of 29 approved by Lt. Gen. Pat White, commander, Combined
Joint Task Force- Operation Inherent Resolve, following a review in accordance with Army and Air
Force regulations. Individual units were responsible for submitting award packages to CJTF-OIR for
service members who had been injured and may be eligible for the Purple Heart as a result of the
ballistic missile attack. 80 packages were received by CJTF-OIR and each package was evaluated
individually by a CJTF-OIR review board in accordance with eligibility criteria contained within Army
Regulation 600-8-22, Chapter 2, Section 2-8, and Air Force Manual 36-2806, Attachment 2,
paragraph A2.10. The review board submitted their recommendations to White for final decision
and determination for U.S. Army soldier Purple Hearts. Two USAF Airmen Purple Heart submissions
were reviewed as part of the CJTF-OIR process and the CJTF-OIR Commander passed his
recommendation against approval for these two awards to the U.S. Air Forces Central Command
Commander, Lt. Gen. Joseph T. Guastella, who was the final approval authority for U.S. Air Force
airmen. It is important to note that a Traumatic Brain Injury (TBI) diagnosis does not automatically
qualify a Service Member for Purple Heart eligibility or awarding, and the CJTF-OIR process was
designed to be a fair and impartial proceeding that evaluated each case in accordance with
applicable regulations. Ultimately, 29 Purple Hearts have been approved for injuries sustained by
U.S. Army soldiers, and the rest of the awards are expected to be presented this week."

Syria, including approximately 2,000 foreign terrorist fighters. The U.S. military provides a range of
support to help ensure the SDF is able to detain these ISIS fighters securely and humanely, including
support to repair and renovate SDF detention facilities, very few of which are purpose-built. We

prosecute, and, where appropriate, to incarcerate them. The United States, working closely with our
Coalition partners, has sufficient forces on the ground and capabilities projected from surrounding
countries to execute the defeat-ISIS campaign in Syria. Although Turkey's October incursion
complicated the northeast Syrian battlespace, we adjusted our force presence and continue to
support our SDF partners. U.S. presence in Syria is bolstered by the Global Coalition of 77 nations
and 5 international organizations, many of which provide funding, military capabilities, law
enforcement coordination, and political support necessary to achieve the enduring defeat of ISIS.
The President has made clear that the United States remains fully committed to the enduring defeat
ofISIS, which is critical to our national security, ISIS remains a threat, and unless pressure is
maintained, a reemergence of ISIS is a very real possibility. We are committed to keeping that from
happening."

* Super-Duper Missile. Barbara Starr, CNN, asked if we had anything we could provide regarding the
President's reference during today's USSF flag ceremony to a Super-Duper Missile. Coordinated with
OUSD(R&E) and provided: "We will not discuss capabilities of any systems we may or may not have
under development. Hypersonics is a characteristic of flight defined generally as flight at speeds in
excess of Mach 5 and up to Mach 25. Fielding hypersonic weapons is a top technical research and
engineering priority, and the United States has a robust program for the development of hypersonic
weapon systems. DOD is pursuing conventional medium- and intermediate-range hypersonic strike
weapons that defeat adversary anti-access/area-denial capabilities and hold adversary maritime,
coastal and deep-inland assets at risk. We are also developing a robust, layered defense to counter
adversary hypersonic weapon capability. Our aim is to transition from development to operational
systems at scale to provide the warfighter with transformational weapons in sufficient quantities to
perform high-priority strike missions."

PRC's malign behavior both privately and publicly, and will continue to do so. We are concerned
by increasing, opportunistic activity by the PRC to coerce its neighbors and press its unlawful
maritime claims in the South China Sea (SCS), while the region and the world is focused on
addressing the COVID-19 pandemic."
* Military Commissions Personnel Moves. Sacha Pfeiffer, NPR, plans to publish a news story about
various OMC- related personnel changes that have occurred over the past few months, including
9/11 defense lawyer Harrington withdrawing, 9/11 Judge Cohen's retirement, and SecDef naming a
new Convening Authority. The reporter requested comment on tape. The interview request was
declined and previously provided statements were reiterated: "The rotation of military members
and leaders is common across the services. Therefore, the Military Commissions process will
continue to follow the Military Commissions Act of 2009, the Manual for Military Commissions, and
the Regulation for Trial by Military Commission - which implement the uniform code of military
justice process for the detail of a military judge and also set forth procedures that guide the
Department of Defense. Like other complex legal proceedings, military commissions do not operate
on a predetermined timeline. Rather the process allows for ensuring a fair commissions process." In
addition, forwarded DOD's release on the new CA:
https://www.defense.gov/Newsroom/Releases/Release/Article/2158184/secdef-appoints-new-

DOD RELEASES
* 5G Network at Nellis. Announced that OUSD(I) and USAFWC at Nellis AFB have teamed to build a
5G cellular network on Nellis as part of DOD's development of 5G for both defense and civilian
uses. Only users taking part in the testing will have access to the private network. The network will
feature relocatable cell towers that can be set up and taken down in less than an hour. Testing will
involve mobile operations centers where team members will use the network while on the move.
Construction on the network at Nellis starts in July; testing at Nellis will start in January 2021 and
continue in three 12-month phases. This test builds upon DoD's previously announced 5G
communications technology prototyping and experimentation at Hill AFB, Utah; Joint Base Lewis-
McChord, Washington; Marine Corps Logistics Base Albany, Georgia; and Naval Base San Diego,
California.
* DPA Title 3 COVID-19 PPE Project. Announced that DoD, in support of HHS, has signed a $2.2
million contract with Hollingsworth & Vose to increase U.S. domestic production of 27.5 million N95
ventilator filters, and 3.1 million N95 respirators per month, starting in August. This investment will
increase U.S. domestic production of N95 mask respirator and mask ventilator filter production by
over 30 million combined over the next 120 days.

* Russian Aircraft in Libya, Ashley Roque, Janes, queried for additional information regarding the
U.S. Africa Command press release that Russia is supplying aircraft in Libya. Coordinated with
AFRICOM to respond: Over multiple days in May, Russian MiG 29s and SU-24 fighters departed
Russia. At that time, all the Russian aircraft have Russian Federation Air Force markings. The newly
unmarked Russian MiG 29s then depart Syria. They are flown by Russian military members and
escorted by Russian fighters based in Syria, head to Libya, landing in Eastern Libya near Tobruk for
fuel. The MiG-29s are then delivered to Al Jufra Air Base in Libya, where they will likely provide close
air support and offensive fires for the Russian state-sponsored Wagner Group. There were at least
14 aircraft delivered.
* Ukraine Security Assistance. Luis Martinez, ABC, asked about Ukraine security assistance.
Responded that we do not speculate on security assistance packages that are under review with
Congress.

* DC Guard Activation / Monuments. Received numerous queries asking for details on the activation
of the DC Guard to protect monuments in the District of Columbia. Provided the following: "On
June 23, at the request of the Secretary of the Interior, and with the approval of the Secretary of
Defense, the Secretary of the Army activated roughly 400 members of the DC National Guard to
support the National Park Police in a civil disturbance and security role around the District. Since
their activation, none of the National Guard members have been dispatched to actual monument
locations to provide assistance to the NPP. They remain on standby at the DC Armory at this time.

National Guard personnel will not be armed, and will serve as a uniformed deterrence and crowd
management capacity to maintain closures and restricted areas."

* Child Development Center Safety and Health Guidance. Karen Jowers, Military Times, asked if the
DOD has issued guidance for child development centers and family child care homes regarding

"The Acting Assistant Secretary of Defense for Manpower and Reserve Affairs issued a

Development Programs during the COVID-19 pandemic. The memorandum followed the Acting
Assistant Secretary of Defense for Manpower and Reserve Affairs Memorandum, issued on March

support and continuity of operations. The department will continue to carry out Defense Secretary
Dr. Mark T. Esper's three priorities for the COVID-19 pandemic response: (1) protect our military

support the whole-of-nation response to the pandemic."

individuals so that they are not on the battlefield killing other Americans or Allied Forces and we can
detain them until the end of hostilities." On background, additionally provided: "DOD prefers that
the detainees not be taken to GTMO or have a military commission trial. DOD does not want to hold
them indefinitely in Iraq or elsewhere -- DOD wants to get out of detention business. DOD is not
preparing to transport them back to US."

* Chinese Spy and Linkedin Vulnerabilities. Katrina Manson, Financial Times, queried about DC-

"DoD personnel are not prohibited from personal use of Linkedin. They are trained in the risks
associated with exposing personal information on social media sites, and obligated to protect any

Department of Defense regularly issues guidance to our workforce regarding the general threats to
DoD personnel posed bythe proliferation of publicly available information and provides guidance on
specific social media platforms, including their websites and smartphone applications." Also
provided, "DoD is always on alert to detect and mitigate threats posed by foreign intelligence
services to recruit our personnel. In addition to our counterintelligence operations, we have a very
robust process for conducting background investigations at various levels depending on a person's
access to classified information. Additionally, we have developed insider threat programs within all
DoD components to provide a venue for early detection of issues that could be indicators of
potential foreign intelligence penetration in our workforce."

* Hong Kong. Ryan Browne, CNN, asked how the National Security Law on Hong Kong is affecting
military relations, specifically ship visits. Responded: "The PRC's decision to unilaterally impose the
National Security Law on Hong Kong follows a pattern of the PRC disregarding its international
commitments and erodes' the high degree of autonomy promised to the Hong Kong people under
the Sino-British Joint Declaration. The Department is taking steps to implement the President's July
14 Executive Order that promotes accountability for those responsible for extinguishing Hong Kong's
freedoms, including a review to update DoD guidance on defense contacts and exchanges with the
Hong Kong Special Administrative Region. The people and government of Hong Kong have a long
history of warmly welcoming visiting U.S. naval vessels, the last being USS Blue Ridge in April 2019."

* Vehicle Donation to Colombia. Flavia Camargos, Shephard Media (London), queried about the
donation of M1117 vehicles to the Colombian Army. Provided: "Colombia was approved in June
2020 for the transfer of up to 200 M1117 vehicles under the Excess Defense Article (EDA) program.
Under the EDA program, administered by DSCA, defense articles declared as excess by the military
departments can be offered to foreign governments or international organizations to support U. S.
allies in their modernization efforts. These vehicles will also assist Colombia with their counter-
narcotics programs. The delivery date is unknown at this point as the case is still in development
and has not yet been finalized. The vehicles were advertised as excess by the U.S. Army to U.S.
partners and allies, The Colombian Army requested to acquire them through the Foreign Military
Sales program process. Colombia was also approved for two C-130Hs in May 2019."

* Afghanistan Drawdown. Several media entities (FOX News, AFP, VOA, Basharq (UAE Regional),
German Press Agency) asked for DoD's statement on Afghanistan force level drawdown following
SECDEF's remarks during a Fox News interview on Saturday night. Provided the cleared RTQ
statement below:
"Over the next four months, the United States will continue to drawdown its force levels in
Afghanistan based on conditions in-country. Our current trajectory expects to see the number of US
service members in Afghanistan be below 5,000 by the end of November. As always, such
drawdowns remain conditions-based and are implemented after consultation with Congress and in
direct coordination with our NATO Allies and partners.
"The U.S. military presence in Afghanistan remains focused on capabilities- not numbers. Projected
force levels will continue to provide commanders with the capabilities and authorities necessary to
protect ourselves, our Allies and partners, and U.S. national interests. We will continue to execute
our counterterrorism mission while simultaneously supporting the 38-nation NATO Resolute Support
Train, Advise, Assist mission and Afghan National Defense and Security Forces (ANDSF) as they work
to secure peace in the country.
"The best path to a lasting peace in Afghanistan remains a negotiated political settlement among
Afghans. The United States continues to honor its commitments under the U.S.-Taliban agreement.
The United States will continue to work to facilitate the start and successful conclusion of intra-
Afghan negotiations —the only process that can produce a lasting peace for Afghanistan."

Force - Operation INHERENT RESOLVE (CJTF-OIR) assessed that unfortunately 105 civilian deaths

Emergency Response Program (CERP) that have not been publicly released. Finally, explained that
the Department does not maintain a publicly-available source providing information on all incidents
that may have led to an offer of a condolence payment, although the recently approved "Interim
Regulations for Condolence or Sympathy Payments to Friendly Civilians for Injury or Loss That Is
Incident to Military Operations" further standardizes such reporting requirements in the future.
Explained that for the safety of the recipients, as well as respect for their privacy, we generally do
not release specific information regarding payments for property damage, personal injury, or death,
including amounts that were paid to recipients.

offensive cyber teams to pursue foreign adversaries in the event an adversary targets U.S. election
systems."

* Proposed MHS Cuts. Meghann Myers, Military Times, requested comment on the Politico article
regarding MHS cuts and the President's tweet. Provided the previously cleared response below and
referred the reporter to the SECDEF statement and ATSD(PA) recent social media comments pushing
back against inaccuracies in reporting: "Like all other parts of the Department, the Military Health
System (MHS) continually assesses how it can most effectively align its assets in support of the
National Defense Strategy. The MHS will not waiver from its mission to provide a ready medical
force and a medically ready force. Any potential changes to the health system will only be pursued
in a manner that ensures its ability to continue to support the Department's operational
requirements and to maintain our beneficiaries' access to quality health care."
* Hypersonic Capabilities. Steve Trimble, Aviation Week, followed up his initial query about whether
DoD was developing nuclear capabilities for hypersonic systems. His questions are based on a line in
an RFI posted to beta.sam gov by the Air Force Nuclear Weapons Center that asked for information
on a "thermal protection system that can support hypersonic glide to ICBM ranges." The reporter is
inferring that, given this line is in an RFl from the AFNWC, that DOD is, in fact, developing nuclear-
capable hypersonic systems, which is in direct contravention of DoD's consistent statement that our
hypersonic systems will be conventional only. The reporter's question following yesterday's
response: "I understand the statement, but there's appears to be an inconsistency between the
policy and the source of the RFI. Can OSD explain why the Air Force Nuclear Warfare Center is
seeking enabling technologies for an intercontinental-range HGV? At the very least, can you say if
the AFNWC's purpose is to seek enabling technology only for conventional HVs? If so, why isn't the
research based at the AFLCMC's program office for conventional hypersonic gliders and cruise
missiles at Eglin AFB?" Coordinated with A/DUSD(R&E), OUSD(R&E) principal director for
hypersonics, SAF/PA and AFNWC/PA and provided: "DOD is not developing nuclear-capable
hypersonic weapons. There are common technology needs between the nuclear enterprise and
hypersonic systems. Particularly in the area of high temperature materials, we typically collaborate
on the development of advanced dual-use materials technology. I will reiterate that our entire
hypersonic program portfolio continues to be based on delivering conventional effects only. For
information on this particular RFI, I will refer you to the Air Force Nuclear Warfare Center."

QUERIES OF NOTE
* Navy's Center for Cost Analysis. Tony Bertuca, Inside the Pentagon, asked several follow up
questions to his previous query regarding a Memorandum of Agreement that CAPE signed with the
Navy to absorb the duties of the Navy's Center for Cost Analysis. Tony referred to Mr. John Whitley's
comments in his nomination hearing August 4. Responded: CAPE does the Major Defense
Acquisition Program cost estimates for the Navy's programs; the MOA has been paused and
modified to reflect new reality with COVID; confirmed that CAPE has joined cost analysts from
NAVAIR to develop the milestone B ICE for the Next Gen Jammer LB and that this is a pilot program
for how this relationship could work in the future and they are using remote work technology
because of COVID; and clarified that CAPE is not planning to pay to do more work to get the Navy's
cost estimates, but that the Navy would provide cost analysts, on a temporary basis, to work with
CAPE to complete ICEs for Navy programs.
* VA and Veteran Suicide Data. Ben Kesling, WSJ, requested comment on the America's Warrior
Partnership report that stated data collected by the VA on veteran suicides undercounted the actual
number. Specifically, he asked if the DoD participated in the project, whether the DoD concurred
with the findings, if the report's findings were factual, and whether the DoD coordinated with the VA
to change its processes for collection. Coordinated the responses with DSPO (OFR) and provided to
the reporter: "The Department provided data to America's Warrior Partnership for this project,
including data to confirm if suicide decedents included in the America's Warrior Partnership
presentation ever served in the U.S. military. As the findings relate to veteran suicide counts, we
defer to the VA for comment. While there are limitations in data reporting at the state level, the
DoD and VA have comprehensive suicide surveillance systems in place to further understand the
scope of the problem. The Department defers to the VA to comment on veteran suicide counts.
While there are limitations in data reporting at the state level, the DoD and VA use multiple data
sources to confirm suicide counts in order to provide the most comprehensive surveillance possible.
The Department defers to the VA to comment on the methodology of or any changes to the VA
suicide surveillance processes. The DoD and VA work together to surveil suicide deaths of Service
members and veterans, and to provide mutual support when changes are needed."
* Military Spouse Employment Partnership. Caitlin Kenney, Stars & Stripes, requested a follow-up on
the Military Spouse Employment Partnership statement on military spouse hiring. Specifically, she
wanted to know if the number of jobs hired correlated with the 22% increase and if there was any

MC&FP (M&RA) and provided to reporter: "More than 31,000 jobs are correlated with the 22%
increase. Reporting is voluntary, so we cannot respond specifically that the spouses were hired in
that timeframe - only that their hires were reported in that timeframe."

* Bob Woodward Book. Sasha Ingber, Newsy, asked for DoD comment to the tape of President

advancements in hypersonics. Provided the following response to the reporter: "Hypersonics is a
characteristic of flight defined generally as flight at speeds in excess of Mach 5. We are focused on
tactical, conventional air-, land- and sea-based hypersonic systems that provide the U.S. warfighter
with a transformational capability. Our aim is to transition from development to operational
systems at scale to provide the warfighter with the highest-quality weapons in sufficient quantities
to perform any mission in support of the National Defense Strategy. We plan to field hypersonics
systems in the early- to mid-2020s starting in 2023.*

possible set of backgrounds, talents, and skills to increase the overall readiness and effectiveness of

*JATF-S. Ronan Farrow, New Yorker Magazine, is working on a "whistleblower story" involving a DOJ official who was assigned to JIATF-5 in August 2017.

drug interdiction targeting. According to the whistleblower, "over 100 alerts" were purposely "illegally mislabeled/disguised" as FBI entries, but were actually

obligation of full disclosure, while denying defendants their right to due process. Three JIATE-S officials (USCG Admiral, USCG CAPT & USAF Coll are accused

free of medical conditions or physical defects that would require excessive time lost from duty for necessary treatment or hospitalization, or would likely

* Military Assistance to Azerbaijan. Joe Gould, Defense News, queried about an increase in DoD
Section 333 security assistance funding in 2018 and 2019 to Azerbaijan. He wanted to know what
was the aid we provided and whether or not a waiver was required. Provided: "DoD assistance to
Azerbaijan is carefully structured so that it will not undermine or hamper ongoing efforts to
negotiate a peaceful settlement between Armenia and Azerbaijan or be used for offensive purposes
against Armenia. Under the DoD Section 333 authority to build capacity in foreign security forces,
DoD is working with Azerbaijan to build its capacity to secure its maritime border and southern
border with Iran and detect and prevent terrorist operations, counter WMD proliferation, and
counter illicit drug trafficking, security objectives that are in the national security interests of both
the U.S. and Azerbaijan. DoD also works to increase Azerbaijan's NATO interoperability and readiness
to support the Resolute Support Mission in Afghanistan through multilateral exercises, professional
military education, subject matter expert exchanges, and English language training, for example.
Through the Cooperative Threat Reduction Program, DoD works to reduce WMD threats against U.S.

for Missing and Exploited Children (NCMEC), to investigate and combat the possession,
dissemination, or production of child pornography. Additionally, the department participated in the
development of the National Strategy for Child Exploitation Prevention and Interdiction alongside
several other agencies, led by the Office of the Deputy Attorney General."

established plans and strategic objectives, and does not equate to a change in U.S. policy or
objectives. He did not take any questions from the press. You can read the full text of his

{"alpha_words": 517, "chars": 3499, "method": "ocr", "page": 233, "variant": "plain-vision"}
* Joint Regional Security Stack. Tony Capaccio, Bloomberg, asked if fielding of the Joint Regional
Security Stack (JRSS) for both the SIPRNET and unclassified network had been paused and what the
outlook is for the program. Coordinated with OSD CIO and OT&E and provided: Fielding of JRSS o
the unclassified network has not been paused. Transition of SIPRURSS to operations on SIPRNET has
been postponed in order to provide additional time to refine the operational requirements, mature
joint tactics, techniques and procedures (TTPs) for mid-point security, implement configuration
changes to installed equipment, and integrate results from ongoing Zero Trust pilots. Fielding of
JRSS on the unclassified network will continue. Per the August 2020 DMI EXCOM's direction, SIPR-
JRSS implementation will be aligned with the SIPR mid-tier security way-ahead, which will be
informed by the outcome of Zero Trust pilots and cybersecurity architecture analyses currently in
progress.

would need the opportunity to review the alleged comments before providing a response to them.
However, successive Secretaries of Defense and Chairmen of the Joint Chiefs have tasked the Office
of Net Assessment to provide various viewpoints-from a blue and red perspective-when assessing
national security policy, The office's insights regarding the importance of a more lethal military and
more capable allies were included in the Department's National Defense Strategy, and their views on
the danger China poses to the United States are repeatedly sought by senior policy makers across
the U.S. government. Mr. Baker and the Office of Net Assessment will continue to provide their best
advice and counsel, regardless of who holds the position of Secretary of Defense."

John Kirby acknowledging receipt of request from FEMA for vaccination support: "The

Novosti; and Dmitry Kirsanov, TASS, asking for a Pentagon comment and specifically if the U.S.

regards to the United States policy towards Libya with specifics. The United States opposes all
foreign military intervention in Libya. We strongly support the October 23 Libyan ceasefire
agreement, including the immediate withdrawal of foreign military forces and mercenaries.
This includes the need for the departure of Russian proxy forces. To that end, the United
States has been a leader on promoting adherence to the arms embargo. We remain fully
committed to ensuring advanced weapons systems neither enter Libya nor are proliferated
from Libya into the hands of dangerous actors, and further stoking violent conflict in violation
of the word and spirit of the arms embargo. The United States prioritizes finding an inclusive,
negotiated, political solution to the Libyan conflict."

* Iraq Announcement of Killing ISIS Deputy Commander. Zachary Halaschak, Washington

Al-Kadhimi announced that Iraqi Secuity Forces killed Abu Yaser al-Issawi, supervisor of ISIS

* Korean Exercises and OPCON. Nari Baek, Yonhap News agency, asked for a comment on the

===== PAGE 255 =====
{"alpha_words": 410, "chars": 2860, "method": "ocr", "page": 255, "variant": "plain-vision"}
ROK PM discussing OPCON transfer and exercises. Referred to Mr. Kirby's comments at the
presser for exercises, and after coordination with OSD(Policy), provided previously cleared
lines on OPCON: "Wartime Operational Control will transition when the mutually-agreed upon
conditions are fully met. A conditions-based wartime OPCON transition is not only what the
United States and Republic of Korea mutually agreed to, it is also necessary to ensure the
security of our military forces, peoples, and the region. A commitment to a specific timeframe
would put our forces and people at risk. Similarly, ensuring the security of military forces,
peoples, and the region requires is more complicated than merely swapping the leadership of
the Combined Forces Command."
* DPAA/PLA Dialogue. Jennifer White, NHK, and Stacy Hsu, Taiwan's Central News Agency,
asked for a comment on the story that said the DoD talked with the PLA this week.
Coordinated with OSD(Policy) and replied with "From January 25-26, 2021, the Defense
POW/MIA Accounting Agency (DPAA) researchers conducted a first-ever Virtual Archival
Exchange with their PRC counterparts at the People's Liberation Army (PLA) Archives
Department. The two-day event was a rare opportunity to seek new information, discuss
previous requests, and raise possible field investigations in the coming year with the PLA
Archives Department. There were 15 U.S. POW/MIA cases specifically discussed covering
World War (WWII), the Korean War, Cold War, and the Vietnam War. DPAA will continue to
engage with the PLA regarding these cases and the U.S. looks forward to safely executing
activities in China once COVID -19 safety protocols and operational planning requirements are
in place."

*JEDI, At 4 p.m. today, the DoD CIO sent an Information Paper on the Upcoming JEDI Cloud
Litigation Milestone to Congress, explaining that the Court of Federal Claims (COFC) will make
a significant ruling within the coming weeks related to the Joint Enterprise Defense
Infrastructure (JEDI) Cloud contract post-award protest. The ruling is on the Government's
Motion to Dismiss, in Part, Count Four of Amazon Web Services (AWS)'s protest complaint.
Count Four relates to AWS's allegation of improper influence at the highest levels of
government that, allegedly, unfairly affected the outcome of the JEDI Cloud competition. The
parties are now waiting for the Court's ruling. After Congressional Notification was complete,
the information paper was sent to DoD and interested trade media.

Commissions provided invitation to media which had expressed an interest in travelling to

Travel is currently planned for Feb. 4.

QUERIES OF NOTE
* Vacancies. Laura Seligman, Politico, asked: "I'm hoping you can tell me who is the new Army No 2 now that
McPherson has moved over - aod also, is there a new acting Navy under?" Response provided: "Under Secretary of
the Army James E. McPherson is still serving in that position and concurrently serving as the Acting Secretary of
the Navy, The Under Secretary of the Navy position is vacant and no one has been designated to fill it in an acting
capacity."
* COVID-19 Readiness Impact. Naveed Jamali, Newsweek, asked if we are using intel assets to preserve readiness
during COVID-19. Provided the following response: "The Department of Defense continuously assesses the
worldwide environment for threats to our military readiness and national defense using a variety of capabilities and
in conjunction with the broader Intelligence Community and our allies and partners."
* CARES Act Funding for IT, Jackson Barnett, FedScoop, asked whether supplemental funding is being used to
develop IT capabilities. Responded that, yes, the CARES Act provided DOD with $300 million that will be used to
help increase IT and teleworking capabilities in support of the ongoing crisis.
* Zoom Policy, Oriana Pawlyk, Military.com, asked about the policy governing the use of Zoom by DOD
personnel, whether employees could use the platform on their personal devices and about other tools employees can
use to collaborate while teleworking. Provided that the department requires our workforce only use DOD-approved
platforms when conducting official business. DOD users may not host meetings using Zoom's free or commercial
offerings. 'Zoom for Government' has been issued a FedRAMP provisional authorization, which allows DOD
component authorizing officials to issue a component-level Authority to Operate, or ATO, using publicly-releasable
DOD information not categorized as For Official Use Only. Further provided that DOD employees are not
prohibited from using Zoom on their personal devices and referred her to the CIO-prepared website listing
teleworking tools available to the DOD workforce.
* JEDI. Sarah Martinson, Law360, asked for comment on a letter from the Information Technology Acquisition
Advisory Council saying that the JEDI contract should be awarded to more than one company. Responded that we
remain confident in our award of the JEDI Cloud contract to Microsoft and continue to focus on getting this critical
capability into the hands of our warfighters as quickly and efficiently as possible.
* Telework. Paul Shinkman, US News & World Report, asked if it was safe to say that more defense employees
and contractors are teleworking on DOD servers now than ever before. Responded: Yes.
* Cyberattacks. Sangmin Lee, Radio Free Asia, asked if the increased telework has led to indications that
adversaries like North Korea have increased cyberattacks? Responded that, because we do not want to provide any
information that might help potential adversaries attack our networks, we will not be able to provide intornation, to
include the specifics of attacks, over what systems and from whom. Referred reporter to the remarks by Mr. Deasy
and Lt. Gen. Shwedo from today's briefing
* Lobbying Provision in NDAA. John Donnelly, CQ Roll Call, asked about a DOD legislative proposal to partly
roll back changes in the FY 18 NDAA that restrict retired senior officials' ability to lobby DOD. b45)
kb)(5)
